When we inspire others, many wonderful things can happen. The Krsna consciousness movement will grow naturally. Plan-making based on the externals of preaching will not be effective. We may build this structure, that structure, and another structure, but if they are not based on a solid core, our preaching will have no substance. There has to be Krsna consciousness in the Krsna consciousness movement.

Prahlada Maharaja is a *bhakta,* and he prays to Lord Nrsimhadeva for there to be good fortune throughout the universe. He did not think only of the welfare of his father, who was envious of him, but of giving Krsna consciousness to everyone in the universe. What an amazing and compassionate absorption he had! He prayed: Let all envious persons be pacified, including my father. His envious father had taken control of the universe and was suffocating everyone. To benefit the universe he prayed that his father’s envy be removed along with the envy of all living beings. “Let them become calm by practicing *bhakti-yoga* so that they will think of one another’s welfare. Then we will all be engaged in the service of the Supreme Transcendence, Lord Sri Krsna, and always remain absorbed in thoughts of Him. ”

In Prahlada’s prayer we see both his internal and external absorption. He has prayed for the welfare of all living beings in the universe and to always be absorbed in thoughts of and engaged in the service of Lord Sri Krsna. Srila Prabhupada’s mood is the same. He didn’t want us to think only of ourselves and therefore to simply go off to the forest to observe *mauna-vrata.* Rather, he recommended that we think of others. Now that our lives had become fortunate, he wanted us to make others fortunate by distributing Krsna consciousness. Certainly we should be careful that in the process we do not lose our own Krsna consciousness, thereby distributing something other than Krsna conscious devotion in the name of distributing Krsna consciousness. We will always have to fight the tendency to become too external, absorbed simply in self-adoration and pride. Distributing Krsna consciousness is not about distributing *our* glories.

Srila Prabhupada often quoted *yare dekha, tare kaha ’krsna’-upadesa (Cc. Madhya* 7. 128): “Everyone you meet, you should tell them about Krsna. ” Lord Caitanya gave this instruction. This is the highest method of worshiping the Supreme Lord and is the most pleasing to Srila Prabhupada. If somehow or other we can please Srila Prabhupada and get his blessings, then success in this life is assured.

**Question: What essential ingredient do we need in order to care spiritually for others?**

If our service attitude is not strongly based on faith in the Krsna conscious process—faith in guru, in Srila Prabhupada, in the ultimate goal of devotional service—it will be difficult to help others attain faith in the devotional process. What it takes to be an effective spiritual leader cannot be trained; we may learn so many management techniques, but if we are absorbed only in the techniques but have no spiritual substance, we cannot give shelter to others. Caring for devotees has to be based on genuine feeling; it is not a show. No one feels sheltered under someone who is simply making a show of caring.

The Eleventh Canto describes that showbottle people create a reality that has no existence beyond their own imaginations because they are filled with duality. Duality means “I have to make a show of giving shelter, because I have to impress someone. ” But if devotees have a genuine desire to help others, then that is a beginning. I say beginning because caring for devotees takes hard work. Still, we cannot expect reciprocation from Krsna unless we make an effort to satisfy Him. Effort means sacrifice, and sacrifice means we give up our personal considerations to serve a higher cause. Srila Prabhupada taught us about sacrifice by his own example, and Krsna says that He personally supports those devotees who voluntarily accept inconvenience in His service, providing what they lack and preserving what they have. Devotees must have faith in Krsna’s words in order to make sacrifices for Him.

When Krsna sees devotees serving in a mood of personal sacrifice, it is natural for Him to reciprocate and give those devotees internal happiness and satisfaction. The devotees then become inspired to make further sacrifice while at the same time feeling more and more humble. Such devotees are qualified to give others shelter.

**Question: Managers have to deal with some unpleasant things like financial crises and other problems, so devotees in general are not inspired to take up those kinds of responsibilities. Why is it so difficult to find people to take on responsibility?**

Management can be a headache, but I will tell you why I took up the responsibility of management for so many years. It is because I see such service in relation to Srila Prabhupada’s mission, and I am one hundred percent convinced that Prabhupada wanted an institution. Institution means headache; there is no way around that. I saw Srila Prabhupada himself accept the headache, and I saw many leaders under Prabhupada accept the same headache. Although it is now twenty-five years since Prabhupada’s departure, many of my godbrothers are still accepting the headaches only as a burden of love for our spiritual master. There is no other reason to engage in management.

Srila Prabhupada wanted to spread Krsna consciousness through an institution. He received this vision from Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura. Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura had given instructions to his own disciples to form a GBC—that is, to organize an institution. Though it was not done in the Gaudiya Math, in 1970 Srila Prabhupada formed a governing body in ISKCON. He worked with it and molded it right up until his departure in 1977. Srila Prabhupada also made it clear in his books what he wanted. Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati had many elevated followers, but they neglected something—something Srila Prabhupada did not neglect. If we really want to appreciate and realize the full glory of what Srila Prabhupada accomplished, we have to take the burden of love. Srila Prabhupada gave us the holy name. He gave us the association of Vaisnavas. He gave many wonderful things. But Prabhupada’s gift to the world is his institution. He asked his followers to please maintain it after he was gone. This is the burden of love he left for us, and any devotee who has the great fortune to share in this burden will receive sweet nectar: Srila Prabhupada’s mercy.

Prabhupada was not inspired to see devotees going into seclusion to chant Hare Krsna. We can observe, for example, how he dealt with Bhakta Richard, a devotee who had come to Prabhupada but who had gone to Radha-kunda to follow the *babajis* by chanting sixty-four rounds a day. Prabhupada’s comment? “Some day, when Bhakta Richard becomes advanced enough, he will leave Radha-kunda and come back to this movement to preach. ” If you *really* want Prabhupada’s mercy, take up some responsibility in his mission.

Prabhupada took big headaches to manage this movement. He dealt with whatever was going on because everyone wanted *only* Prabhupada to address their problems. At the same time, he translated the *Bhagavatam,* gave class almost every day, and did an incredible amount of preaching. When his disciples saw how much Prabhupada was doing for them, they wanted to somehow relieve his burden. If we do not still want to help, we are missing out on his mercy.

But we should not take on so much responsibility that we are forced to sacrifice our *sadhana.* Prabhupada did not want that either. We have to be convinced that if we are Krsna conscious, everything will somehow get done. Prabhupada wanted us to take up the burden of love he gave us, but not at the expense of our spiritual lives. Prabhupada was always present, and whenever he saw his spiritual sons or daughters over-burdened, he acted like a loving father.

**Question: What did Srila Prabhupada expect of the future preachers and leaders of this movement?**

Srila Prabhupada wrote a letter that covers several important points: the qualifications for leadership, his expectations for his leaders, and what he expected life in the temple to be like. If we read this letter carefully and understand Srila Prabhupada’s mood in writing it, it will help us tremendously in our responsibilities as leaders in this Society. The letter was written in 1972 to Karandhara and addresses centralization.

Srila Prabhupada begins by speaking about the dangers of centralization, but then goes on to explain that if we are not going to centralize, then responsibility for the movement will rest with individual leaders. When he spoke about centralization, he was referring to financial centralization. Usually he encouraged the leaders to come together to discuss ways to expand the movement and to apply his teachings—thus encouraging centralization. This letter was written at a time when several devotees were thinking about centralizing some of the temples and forming one structure, with the other temples being financially dependent on that structure. Srila Prabhupada was disturbed by this thinking. He wrote:

Regarding your points about taxation, corporate status, etc.,

I have heard from Jayatirtha you want to make big plan for centralization of management, taxes, monies, corporate status, bookkeeping, credit, like that. I do not at all approve of such plan.

Do not centralize anything. Each temple must remain independent and self-sufficient. That was my plan from the very beginning, why you are thinking otherwise? Once before you wanted to do something centralizing with your GBC meeting, and if I did not interfere the whole thing would have been killed. Do not think in this way of big corporation, big credits, centralization—these are all nonsense proposals. Only thing I wanted was that books printing and distribution should be centralized, therefore I appointed you and Bali Mardan to do it. Otherwise, management, everything, should be done locally by local men. Accounts must be kept, things must be in order and lawfully done, but that should be each temple’s concern, not yours. Krishna Consciousness Movement is for training men to be independently thoughtful and competent in all types of departments of knowledge and action, not for making bureaucracy. Once there is bureaucracy the whole thing will be spoiled. There must be always individual striving and work and responsibility, competitive spirit, not that one shall dominate and distribute benefits to the others and they do nothing but beg from you and you provide. No. Never mind there may be botheration to register each centre, take tax certificate each, become separate corporations in each state. That will train men how to do these things, and they shall develop reliability and responsibility, that is the point. I am little observing now, especially in your country, that our men are losing their enthusiasm for spreading on our programmes of Krishna Consciousness movement. Otherwise, why so many letters of problems are coming, dissatisfied? That is not a very good sign. The whole problem is they are not following the regulative principles, that I can detect. Without this, enthusiasm will be lacking. Even mechanically following, and if he gets gradually understanding from the class, he will come to the point of spontaneous enthusiasm.

When Srila Prabhupada writes about the regulative principles here, he is not simply referring to the four regulative principles.

Rather, he is talking about the regulative principles of *bhakti-yoga.* He writes:

This spontaneous loving devotional service is not so easy matter, but if one simply sticks strictly to the rules and regulations, like rising early, chanting 16 rounds, chanting gayatri, keeping always clean—then his enthusiasm will grow more and more, and if there is also patience and determination, one day he will come to the platform of spontaneous devotion, then his life will be perfect.

All of this I have told you in Nectar of Devotion. So I do not think the leaders are themselves following, nor they are seeing the others are following strictly. That must be rectified at once. Each centre remain independent, that’s all right, but the president and other officers must themselves follow and see the others are following the regulative principles carefully, and giving them good instruction so they may understand nicely why this tapasya is necessary. And GBC and Sannyasis will travel and see the officers are doing this, and if they observe anything lowering of the standard, they must reform and advise, or if there is some discrepancy I shall remove it. Of course, if new men are coming, they may not be expected immediately to take to our regulative principles cent per cent. Therefore we should not be so anxious to induce them to live in the temple. Anyone who lives in the temple must agree to follow the rules and regulations without fail. So if some new man moves in with us he may become discouraged if he is forced in this way. Therefore let them live outside and become gradually convinced in the class why they should accept some austerity, then they will live with us out of their own accord and follow nicely everything.

It is very difficult to give up very quickly so many bad habits as you have got in your country, so educate them gradually, first with chanting, and do not be so much anxious to count up so many numbers of new devotees, if such devotees go away later being too

early forced. I want to see a few sincere devotees, not many false devotees or pretenders.

So my point is that the regulative principles must be followed by everyone. Otherwise their enthusiasm dwindles and they again think of sex and become restless, and so many problems are there. There is some symptom of missing the point. The point is to be engaged in doing something for Krishna, never mind what is that job, but being so engaged in doing something very much satisfying to the devotee that he remains always enthusiastic. He will automatically follow the regulative principles because they are part of his occupational duty—by applying them practically as his occupational duty, he realizes the happy result of regulative principles. So the future of this Krishna Consciousness movement is very bright, so long the managers remain vigilant that 16 rounds are being chanted by everyone without fail, that they are all rising before four morning, attending mangal arati—our leaders shall be careful not to kill the spirit of enthusiastic service, which is individual and spontaneous and voluntary. They should try always to generate some atmosphere of fresh challenge to the devotees, so that they will agree enthusiastically to rise and meet it. That is the art of management: to draw out spontaneous loving spirit of sacrificing some energy for Krishna. But where are so many expert managers? All of us should become expert managers and preachers. We should not be very much after comforts and become complacent or self-contented. There must be always some *tapasya,* strictly observing the regulative principles— Krishna Consciousness movement must be always a challenge, a great achievement to be gained by voluntary desire to do it, and that will keep it healthy. So you big managers now try to train up more and more some competent preachers and managers like yourselves. Forget this centralizing and bureaucracy. (Letter to Karandhara—Bombay, December 22, 1972)

Srila Prabhupada gives a number of clear directives to ISKCON’s leaders in this letter. He writes, “The spontaneous enthusiasm will come if the devotees are strictly following the basic principles. There must be patience and determination, and then one day, if a devotee continues to perform these activities, he will come to this platform of spontaneous devotion, and his life will be perfect. ” Obviously, Srila Prabhupada expected his disciples to come to the platform of spontaneous devotional service, and he has given us the formula how to achieve it. We should not think that spontaneous devotional service means we can do anything we want in the name of devotional service. Rather, it is a stage of devotion that is solidly based on following the regulative principles and grounded in a pure service attitude.

Prabhupada writes that he can see that the leaders are not strictly following the regulative principles. He attributed the devotees’ dissatisfaction and dwindling enthusiasm to this weakness in the leadership. He expected the leaders to rise early, chant their rounds in the morning, regularly chant their Gayatri mantras, be clean, and attend the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class. All of these items constitute Srila Prabhupada’s most emphasized instructions.

Srila Prabhupada writes in one Fifth Canto purport, “Association is very meaningful; therefore ISKCON tries to perfect one who enters the society. The members of this society should always remember that the society is not like a free hotel. All the members should be very careful to execute their spiritual duties so that whoever comes will automatically become a devotee and will be able to return back to Godhead in this very life. ” *(Bhag.* 5. 8. 30, purport)

Prabhupada also speaks about voluntarily accepting *tapasya.* Although he writes that the temple leaders must strictly follow and see that others are strictly following the regulative principles carefully, and giving good instructions so that they may understand why *tapasya* is necessary, he also writes that not everyone is ready to accept this *tapasya.* We should not be quick to force people to move into the temples if they are not ready to perform *tapasya.* Prabhupada says that they can come regularly and hear the *Bhagavatam* class. Gradually, the desire to follow will manifest in their hearts. Do not be quick to increase the number of devotees living in the temple.

Prabhupada is emphasizing the voluntary spirit here, and how the leaders should become expert in creating a healthy atmosphere where such a voluntary spirit to perform devotional service is cultivated. A leader should be careful not to kill the spirit of enthusiastic service, which is individual, spontaneous, and voluntary.

Krsna conscious preachers have to awaken the voluntary spirit of surrender in others by both preaching and example. Srila Prabhupada did not expect the managers to manage as if ISKCON were a corporation. What he really expected was that devotees would be taught and encouraged to chant sixteen rounds, rise early, attend *mangala-arati,* and attend the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class.

**Question: How important is organized preaching?**

Americans are especially skeptical about organized religion. They feel that when religion becomes too organized, its essence is lost. Many people have been frustrated by organizations without substance. Of course, Srila Prabhupada wanted both organization and substance. He often told us we should take the organizational abilities of the West and combine them with the spiritual vision of the East. India, he said, was like a lame man, and the West was blind. If the lame man could climb on the shoulders of the blind man, then together they could progress.

Similarly, we can make spiritual progress when we have both organization and spiritual vision. Prabhupada wanted us to become organized, and many times he encouraged us in that direction with statements like, “My American and European disciples come from wealthy backgrounds and should use their experience, and with the wisdom of the East, spread Krsna consciousness all over the world. ”

Prabhupada was the original organizer of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. First, he inspired people to practice Krsna consciousness, and then when they came forward enthusiastically, he directed them how to dovetail their skills to spread Krsna consciousness.

Because ISKCON in not a centralized movement, there are many ways to organize the preaching according to time, place, and circumstance. Some of these ways are effective and some not, but none of them are in themselves enough to successfully spread Krsna consciousness. We must also have spiritual vision. If we start with spiritual vision and then preach to give Krsna consciousness to others, then the inspiration how to organize will follow.

Prahlada Maharaja instructs his classmates that in the same ways that one sees himself and takes care of himself, one should use his energies for devotional service to the Supreme Lord, who is the Supersoul in the hearts of all living beings.

Because we tend to be self-centered, we think of ourselves before thinking of others. Prabhupada explains that philanthropists, who apparently think only about others, also think about themselves first; because they think, “I am part of the others, and I will benefit if I take care of them. ” This is a selfish motivation. It is very difficult to find true selflessness in the material world.

Devotees personally experience that when they practice one-pointed devotional service, Krsna takes better care of them than they can take care of themselves. Krsna gives us everything we need to help us be satisfied in any condition of life. Without His grace, we can never be satisfied, even if we appear to have everything we need. Krsna is actually the source of our satisfaction.

**Question: What causes preachers to gradually become more external in their preaching?**

Preachers become more external when they begin to neglect their *sadhana.* They think, “I have more important responsibilities to do than hearing and chanting with the devotees. If I take care of my responsibilities, I will feel good about myself. It’s okay to neglect my hearing and chanting and only go to class once a week. I have more important things to do. And these important things enter my mind as soon as I wake up in the morning. How can I chant *japa* in the morning? I have to think about my duties first and make my plans for the day. I’ll chant *japa* later. ”

But Srila Prabhupada explains that if the devotees do not follow strictly, they will lose their perspective. If they are not themselves enthusiastic to practice the regulative principles of *bhakti-yoga,* how will they inspire others to become enthusiastic? Instead they find themselves becoming more and more dissatisfied. That’s why Srila Prabhupada wrote in this letter that the devotees had big plans to centralize ISKCON—which would include bureaucracy, sitting behind a desk thinking, “Now I have a position and everyone will surrender to me. I’ll control the money and make everyone dependent on me. Everyone will respect me, because I have a desk and an office. ” Prabhupada saw this mentality and smashed it. He understood the root of the problem and why the devotees were losing enthusiasm. He then sarcastically implied, “Where are all these big managers now? Now that I’ve smashed you, where are you? Become big managers on the right platform. And when you are the right kind of big managers, train up more competent preachers. Then the spontaneous enthusiasm will return and the devotees will be inspired. ” It is not that Srila Prabhupada in any way smashed big plans or big ideas for spreading Krsna consciousness, but he never wanted the devotees to preach by sacrificing the most basic Krsna conscious principles.

Of course, there were exceptions. For example, when Srila Prabhupada wanted all seventeen volumes of the *Caitanya-caritamrta* produced in two months, the devotee artists sat in front of their paintings until they fell asleep. Then another set of artists came in to complete the work. All the devotees working on this marathon made many sacrifices. Some of them sacrificed their *sadhana to* get the *yajna* done. Prabhupada certainly gave a large project for his disciples to accomplish—to publish seventeen books in two months—but although some devotees gave up their regulated hearing and chanting during that time, Prabhupada did not expect them to continue serving without *sadhana* once the *yajna* was over.

Thus under certain circumstances, devotees may take on a service that requires much more time than their usual services, and may therefore sacrifice some of their *sadhana* for a few days or a week. But not performing one’s *sadhana* should not become a way of life for managers or anyone else. Managers in particular must be careful to protect their *sadhana.* As soon as a manager thinks, “I have too many important things to do besides my *sadhana, ”* he or she has contracted a disease. What is the disease? Thinking oneself a “big, big manager” with too many things to do instead of hearing and chanting. Such a mentality puts managers into serious danger. And we see that under the stress and pressure of management, devotees with weak *sadhana* lose their enthusiasm.

Another aspect of good management is delegation. Srila Prabhupada did not want us only to become expert managers and preachers, but to train other enthusiastic preachers and managers. Devotees can enthusiastically share responsibility if they are hearing *Srimad-Bhagavatam* nicely, chanting their rounds together, attending *mangala-arati* together, and chanting enthusiastically in *kirtana.* Enthusiasm naturally leads us to render service and to therefore assist Srila Prabhupada in establishing and expanding his mission. Prabhupada said in this letter, “I am a little observing now, especially in your country, that our men are losing their enthusiasm for spreading our programs of Krsna consciousness. ” Again, enthusiasm begins with spiritually strong leadership.

We have to think of how to inspire the devotees. Prabhupada says that the leaders should always try to generate an atmosphere of fresh challenge so that the devotees will enthusiastically rise to meet it. This is the art of management—to draw out the spontaneous loving spirit of sacrificing for Krsna. Srila Prabhupada wanted to empower his leaders; he did not want them to become crippled or disempowered.

**Question: Sometimes the leaders feel crippled and disempowered, thinking that the only time the devotees are enthusiastic is when the**

*sannyasis* **and gurus come to their temple. As soon as these persons leave, everyone loses enthusiasm. What can be done?**

This is why Srila Prabhupada speaks about empowering the local leaders. It is their duty to inspire the devotees after the *sannyasis* and GBC members leave. Empowerment comes from strictly following the basic Krsna conscious activities.

Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura quotes the first verse of Sri *Isopanisad* in his *Bhaktyaloka: vaco-vegam manaso krodha-vegam.* In his commentary he explains that when we control the mind and senses, we first control ourselves and then can control the world. If we control the mind and senses, we will be peaceful and develop taste in Krsna consciousness. It is natural that others will approach such a peaceful devotee and ask how they can also become peaceful. In other words, it is very easy to control others and to be empowered in Krsna consciousness when we have a taste for Krsna consciousness. Krsna consciousness is contagious.

Prabhupada compared Krsna consciousness to an infectious disease. When a person has a contagious infection, others can catch it. Similarly, when we have the infectious disease of taste for hearing and chanting, others around us will also become infected and the disease will spread. This is what empowerment is. Empowerment has nothing to do with having a big position.

**Question: What is the difference between preaching forcefully while thinking of someone’s welfare and preaching out of selfdeception?**

If we are actually thinking of someone else’s welfare, we will think about how to convince that person that we are concerned. We cannot simply use a stick and force someone to do what we think they should do. We have to preach in a way that we try to help them understand. That means that we first have to win his or her trust.

In the *Madhurya-kadambini,* Srila Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura explains that faith can be awakened in two ways: spontaneously, as a result of coming in contact with devotional service in a previous life, or by the forceful preaching of another devotee. Preachers can be “forceful” by their purity, causing others to *want* to surrender to Krsna.

Although we may think of another’s welfare, we cannot justify any mistreatment of that person. If we preach without attachment, and if a person does not accept what we are offering, we have to be detached enough to accept that and not justify forcing the person to bend to our will. Preachers feel responsible for others’ welfare, but they also control their own anger, envy, lust, greed, and their attachment to the results of their preaching. Only then can the preachers truly think of others’ welfare.

**Question: How can we help deviant leaders and devotees who are struggling in their Krsna consciousness or who have lost faith?**

We always have to understand that if any devotee—leaders or not—is having difficulty executing *sadhana* due to some spiritual weakness, it is always in that devotee’s best interest to have the person from whom he is most able to hear speak to him. Devotees are spiritually weak for a reason, and we ourselves may not be able to get to the root of it. But if we find someone else who can, we are acting in the troubled devotee’s best interest. Otherwise, such exchanges can turn nasty, *avaisnava* and political, and the results can be quite unpalatable.

Generally, if a devotee is spiritually weak it is because he or she is uninspired. Whether we are performing *vaidhi-sadhana-bhakti* or *raganuga-sadhana-bhakti* makes no difference; we still need inspiration from the guru’s instructions and the scripture, or from the attraction of service itself.

And we have to be careful in our dealings. When troubled devotees return to the association of devotees, it is important that they do not feel judged. We tend to make casual remarks: “Oh, you are not chanting? You never come to the programs. Why not?”

Immediately they feel judged. We have to know that behind the outward behavior is a devotee’s whole life and all the reasons why he or she has not been coming. If we judge, the devotee will feel, “I don’t need to feel judged every time I come. ” He or she will then gradually stop coming altogether, thinking that our association is unnecessary. When devotees have been distanced from devotional service, they do not need to hear that they are fallen. That does not inspire them.

Sometimes we use others’ experiences to preach, telling everyone what their *maya* is. But if the troubled persons are sincere, they probably already recognize their own *maya.* What they need to hear is about *yogamaya,* the Lord’s internal potency. To the degree that they become attracted to Krsna, to that degree they will become inspired.

Narada Muni told Vyasa, “My dear Vyasa, even though a devotee of Lord Krsna sometimes falls down somehow or other, he certainly does not undergo material existence like others \[fruitive workers, etc. \] because a person who has once relished the taste of the lotus feet of the Lord can do nothing but remember that ecstasy again and again. ” *(Bhag.* 1. 5. 19) Our business as preachers is to somehow or other give others a taste for devotion to Krsna. It was by giving this taste that Lord Caitanya rescued us. Taste should come first. Forget about passing judgment and cutting away attachments. Inspire the devotees with a taste of devotional service.

Of course, sometimes the spiritual master has to correct his disciples and must judge them. That is the spiritual master’s duty. If he does not correct his disciples, the disciples might deviate. Still, it is his primary duty to somehow help his disciples by awakening their natural taste for Krsna consciousness.

Devotees have to become a touchstone by which others can be changed. Prabhupada explains that that this quality is the real test of a Vaisnava. The change of heart is manifest when one becomes attracted to Krsna, Krsna’s devotees, and to Krsna consciousness in general. Having once awakened this attraction, we should always be careful to see that it is never extinguished.

Leaders should feel partly responsible for the Krsna consciousness of all their dependents. If someone lacks Krsna consciousness, the leaders should think, “Maybe there is something I should be doing to increase my own Krsna consciousness. ” Otherwise, we will simply see others’ faults. At the same time, the devotees lacking Krsna consciousness should not blame the leaders for it. We are each responsible for ourselves.

**Question: What qualifications does a leader of a** *nama-hatta* **need?**

In *Godruma Kalpatavi,* Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s prospectus on *nama-hatta,* the Thakura gives nice details about the qualifications of those who will take part in a *nama-hatta.* Actually, the description of the qualifications is very short, and it follows a long description of the disqualifications. Bhaktivinoda Thakura took great pains to emphasize what would disqualify us from participating in the marketplace of the holy name.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes that the main qualification for one taking part in a *nama-hatta* is that he or she be a pure devotee. Hearing that, we might say, “I guess someone else will have to give the *nama-hatta* classes, because I am not a pure devotee. ” However, Bhaktivinoda Thakura qualifies his statement by defining “pure”:

Qualification: whether *brahmana, ksatriya, vaisya, sudra* or lower, or whether *sannyasi, brahmacari, grhastha,* or *vanaprastha,* if one has pure devotion, he can become an employee of the *nama-hatta,* and take up any of the posts....

(Eradication of all material desire, freedom from *jnana* and karma, and favorable service to Krsna comprise pure devotion. According to the devotion in one’s heart one has qualification, and according to the devotion there will be freedom from disqualifications. ) (Sri Sri *Godruma Kalpatavi,* p. 23)

Therefore there are varying degrees of qualification, and to the degree our heart is filled with devotion, to that degree we will be qualified to participate in a nama-hatta.

The *nama-hatta* leader should also discipline himself to make tangible plans for the welfare of his dependents. For instance, Krsna instructed Uddhava and Uddhava then instructed Vidura. When Vidura understood in his discussions with Uddhava that Krsna had been thinking of him, he was overwhelmed by love of God.

Vidura was overwhelmed by the ecstasy of love when he understood that Lord Krsna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, thought of him at the last moment. Although he thought of himself as insignificant, he was remembered by the Lord, by His causeless mercy. Vidura accepted this as a great favor, and thus he cried. ” *(Bhag.* 3. 4. 35, purport)

We feel sheltered when we know someone is thinking of our welfare.

**Question: What suggestions would you have for conducting a** *nama-hatta?*

First, you have to inspire the attendees. Some programs make arrangements for each devotee to prepare a presentation for the week, and the devotees presenting rotate. The presentation may be short—just a few minutes long—and can perhaps discuss something the person has been studying. In the programs I have attended that are run like that, I have seen that everyone has some impetus to participate rather than always being the audience. Part of the program can include memorizing important *slokas.*

Somehow or other you have to get people to fix their minds on Krsna. That should be an important objective of your *nama-hatta* programs; and people should learn something by attending. It is the responsibility of the organizers to keep the *nama-hatta* programs from becoming stagnant. If they are not focused on preaching and bringing in new people, then they have to focus on cultivating those who are already coming. When the attendees are inspired, they will want to do more.

**Question: What are the restrictions regarding preaching to the opposite sex?**

Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes:

(A man’s present, not previous, character is to be considered.

To whatever extent a person can destroy his disqualifications, he obtains shelter of pure devotional service, and receives qualification).

... For a *grhastha* or *vanaprastha* to go to prostitutes, women other than one’s wedded wife, or to engage in unrestricted sex-life with one’s own wife, is forbidden. *Brahmacaris* and *sannyasis* are absolutely restricted from association with any woman. If any man is guilty of the offenses, then he is disqualified from becoming an employee of the *nama-hatta. "*(Sri Sri *Godruma Kalpatavi,* p. 24)

Even in preaching there should not be unrestricted association between the sexes. Householder men should not take care of women, what to speak of women coming under the care of *brahmacaris* or *sannyasis.* Rather, the householders’ wives should care for the women who attend the *nama-hatta* programs. Or, under certain circumstances, the couple can care for them together. Similarly, the women should not take care of the men who come.

Maintaining these restrictions keeps the atmosphere pure. If the atmosphere is pure, we can advance in Krsna consciousness. But if the atmosphere becomes contaminated by subtle desires, then the mind will always be disturbed and we will be unable to give others shelter. The standards about how the sexes should interact should be practiced both in the temple and at home programs. Bhaktivinoda Thakura continues:

Women who are pure devotees can become traveling women salesmen for distributing the holy name among other women, but they cannot give the name to men. According to time, place, and circumstances, however, and with great care, a cautious and mature woman can distribute the name to men. Apart from elderly women or very young girls, men preachers should avoid discussions with women. (Sri Sri *Godruma Kalpatavi,* p. 24)

**Question: How important is it that everyone preach?**

The Bhagavatam explains that a *brahmana* who gains knowledge but does not use it to benefit others is like a clay pot with a crack in it. If you fill a cracked pot with water, the water will leak out. If we fill ourselves with knowledge by hearing, our knowledge will leave us if we do not repeat what we have heard. We should never think that our repeating what we know will empty us.

Lord Caitanya says, “I am the only gardener. If I do not distribute these fruits, what shall I do with them? How many fruits can I alone eat? ” (Cc. *Adi* 9. 37) Therefore, He asked everyone to take the fruits, eat them, and to help Him distribute them. The storehouse is unlimited and the fruits will never be depleted. If we are willing to help the Lord by preaching, we will be able to achieve Him and remember Him at the time of death.

To be able to preach, we must chant purely. What makes devotees powerful is not simply their words but their shining examples. It is how the devotees exemplify the teachings that makes people want to follow them. Sanatana Gosvami said, “Some people preach, but don’t behave nicely. Some people behave nicely, but don’t preach. But, you, Haridasa, both preach and behave perfectly. Therefore you will become a spiritual master of the whole world. ” Both preaching and pure behavior must be there. We must be an example of what we preach if we wish to be potent and pure in our preaching.

**Question: If we do not have someone from whom we are taking shelter, it will be difficult for us to provide others with shelter. What should we do if we are already providing shelter for others despite a lack of shelter ourselves?**

Those who are under someone’s shelter have certain symptoms. Accepting shelter does not necessarily mean that we have someone we always go to with our problems. But we do require shelter if we want to know how to deal with our own problems. That shelter can come in the form of Vaisnava association, the strength of the holy name, through reading Srila Prabhupada’s books, or by remembering the guru’s instructions. The symptom we manifest if we have shelter is that we are able to face adversity without disturbance and can go on serving Krsna without material considerations under the shelter of the holy name. Being sheltered is not artificial; it is a heart experience. When devotees feels sheltered in the heart, they can serve regardless of what happens in their lives and think of the others’ welfare despite their own suffering. They can also chant in a humble state of mind, thinking themselves lower than straw in the street, more tolerant than a tree, ready to offer all respect to others, and not wanting respect for themselves. Those who do not feel such shelter should seek it out.

## Responsibility

## Householders

Householders are meant to derive pleasure from Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s *sankirtana* movement and from hearing and chanting about Krsna. Such activity keeps them spiritually healthy and helps them overcome material obstacles. When Kurma the *brahmana* wanted to renounce household life and join Caitanya Mahaprabhu, the reason he gave was that he faced many problems in household life and because of the struggle he was undergoing to maintain his family. But Caitanya Mahaprabhu told him how to engage himself properly in devotional service, without material entanglement: Simply tell everyone you meet about Krsna, and you will find that your household life provides no obstacles.

Similarly, when Gadadhara Pandita wanted to go with Lord Caitanya, the Lord told him that he was breaking religious principles by giving up his duties. He considered Gadadhara’s desire to travel with the Lord sense gratification. Gadadhara had taken *ksetra-sannyasa* and had made a vow to worship Gopinatha. The Lord told him that he would have been committing two sinful acts by leaving Puri: engaging in sense gratification and giving up his duties. Therefore Lord Caitanya sent him back.

Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura compares household life to a well and gives the example of a king hunting in a forest. The king realized he was being stalked by a ferocious tiger that could kill and devour him. He therefore decided to hide in a well until the tiger went away. He grabbed two branches and lowered himself into the well. But as he lowered himself, he looked down and saw that the well was completely dry and filled with poisonous snakes.

The tiger represents death and the poisonous snakes the anxieties of household life.

What was the king to do? Here he was, holding onto two branches. He could not come out of the well, and he could not settle on the bottom. The two branches represent his pious and impious activities. The branches were beginning to shake because he was in such a tenuous situation. Looking up the king saw two mice chewing on the branches. These mice represent day and night or the passage of time. Without understanding how time is passing, the householder engages in pious and impious activities. Gradually the branches become thin, almost bitten through, and the householder becomes nervous. How will he be saved?

Looking still further up, the king saw a honeycomb in one of the other branches of the tree. Because the king was shaking, the honey began to flow. The king stuck out his tongue and relished the taste of the honey.

Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura says that this is the pleasure of household life. Time is passing, death is inevitable, we are holding onto our pious and impious activities, but despite our distress, we are still trying to taste a drop of honey. Such small sense gratification and so much trouble! A devotee aspires for something higher.

The householders in Lord Caitanya’s *sankirtana* movement are glorious, because they have a higher goal than bodily maintenance. They maintain body and soul simply to support their higher purpose. The Bhagavatam (1. 2. 10) instructs:

*kamasya nendriya-pritir*  
*labho jiveta yavata*  
*jivasya tattva-jijnasa*  
*nartho yas ceha karmabhih*

“Life’s desires should never be directed toward sense gratification. One should desire only a healthy life, or self-preservation, since a human being is meant for inquiry about the Absolute Truth. Nothing else should be the goal of one’s works. ”

Devotees do not find physical maintenance inspiring. Still, they can take care of themselves and their families if they have the vision that such maintenance serves a spiritual purpose. Life passes quickly; we should not allow ourselves to waste time.

Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura states that householders have the right to maintain themselves through performance of their prescribed duties:

Preachers should maintain themselves through honest means. Those householders who are capable of working have no right to beg for their material subsistence, but *brahmacaris* and *sannyasis* should beg to maintain their body and should not take up any other means as an alternative. Wherever the preacher goes in the course of his activities, he should always be alert as to how to increase the preaching of the holy name at that particular place.

(Sri Sri *Godruma Kalpatavi,* p. 24)

But if someone voluntarily offers something to reciprocate what we have given, we may accept it.

Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura also explains that householders can join the *nama-hatta.* But they should earn an honest livelihood, and when it comes time to distribute the holy name or to preach, they should not do it as a way to “get rice and milk. ” They should earn their livelihood first, and then distribute the holy name and Krsna conscious literature after that.

**Question: How can we balance spiritual life and the material duties we have to perform?**

Most devotees who have to work experience the pressures and demands of having to support themselves and their families. It is usually not easy to support a family, and we may come to a point where it becomes practically impossible to tolerate the pressure. But if we want to find a balance, we have to be aspiring for something beyond bodily maintenance. Otherwise there is no question of balance; we are all one-sided on the material side. If we base all our goals on the misconception that we are the body and everything we want should maintain the body, then everything we do will have a false conclusion.

Those who lack faith will find it especially hard to find a balance. This is because they will neglect their spiritual duties under pressure to perform their material duties. Those who identify with the desire to attain service at Krsna’s lotus feet will be protected by their faith. Balance means *sadhana.*

**Question: Can such spiritual duties include preaching?**

Preaching is important, but if we do not practice *sadhana,* how can we preach? Prabhupada named our three priorities: health, *sadhana,* and preaching. Although preaching is important, *sadhana* is essential. Otherwise, what would we be preaching?

*Sadhana* refers to the activities we perform every day that help us remember our goal to satisfy the Supreme Lord and to remember Him always. Included in *sadhana* is chanting, hearing, and remembering.

Those who have become too absorbed in material activities and have lost their ability to practice *sadhana* should seek the association of devotees who can inspire them to take it up again. This is the proper way to balance life. If we balance material life with proper *sadhana,* then it will be natural to preach as we have time—and such preaching will be real preaching, not false preaching.

Srila Prabhupada did not start preaching immediately in his life either. Rather, he underwent many difficulties in his business, and he often said that it took him years to fulfill his spiritual master’s order that he preach in the English language. Still, he was always preparing to perform this service, and meditation on his guru’s order became his daily meditation and thus part of his *sadhana.* Srila Prabhupada was *nitya-siddha,* a devotee who came from the spiritual world to teach by his own example how to give up household life and dedicate one’s life fully to the Lord’s service. Sometimes the *nitya-siddhas* descend to teach many things—how to live as surrendered householders among them.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura was also a *nitya-siddha* householder with many children and domestic responsibilities. He too taught us to live a Krsna conscious life.

If we maintain our *sadhana,* giving it the highest priority in our lives, and perform all our other domestic responsibilities as secondary to that, we will be able to preach. Although Prabhupada gave us the priorities of health, then *sadhana,* then preaching, he did not mean that we should devote our whole life to caring for health. He meant that if we are ill we should seek healing. We should not use curable ill health as an excuse to not engage in Krsna consciousness. Rather, we should endeavor to improve our health, understanding that our body is Krsna’s body. Then we must perform *sadhana,* and then preach. That is how to balance life. It is not necessary to renounce everything in order to do these things. If you are in the household *asrama,* then stay there, but chant the Lord’s holy name every day with faith, and make the name the center of your life.

**Question: How can we relieve the mind of the anxieties of household duties and responsibilities?**

New householders rarely understand the full weight of their responsibilities. They usually have a narrow and idealistic conception of what household life means. As the picture opens and their responsibilities unfold, however, they begin to feel the anxiety of their responsibilities. The only thing they can do is to find shelter in Krsna consciousness.

Even the nondevotees suffer anxiety. But they have other methods to give themselves relief: temporary sense gratification and intoxication. Devotees seek relief in Krsna consciousness; they give up lower tastes to attain something higher.

But why do we struggle to find shelter? It is because the mind tells us that there is shelter elsewhere. We still do not have faith in taking shelter in Krsna consciousness. Although we faithfully perform our *sadhana* out of a sense of obligation, and this is a good beginning, we have to go beyond mere obligation. Krsna says (Bg. 12. 9):

*atha cittam samadhatum*  
*na saknosi mayi sthiram*  
*abhyasa-yogena tato*  
*mam icchaptum dhananjaya*

“My dear Arjuna, O winner of wealth, if you cannot fix your mind upon Me without deviation, then follow the regulative principles of *bhakti-yoga.* In this way develop a desire to attain Me. ”

Krsna uses the phrase, *abhyasa-yoga. Abhyasa-yoga* refers to the regulated practice of fixing the mind exclusively on Krsna. A benefit of fixing the mind at the lotus feet of Nanda Maharaja’s divine son is that we become fearless in this world and no longer worry about the outcome of our present activities.

Until we are practicing pure *bhakti,* we can perform either *niskama-karma-yoga* or *sakama-karma-yoga. Niskama-karma-yoga* means to execute *karma-yoga* with *jnana,* knowledge. Those practicing *niskama-karma-yoga* attempt to give up their material desires by becoming detached from the results of their activities; they offer the fruits of their activities to Krsna. *Sakama-karma-yoga* means to perform *karma-yoga* to fulfill one’s material desires—that is, to keep the fruits—although out of piety, one tithes some of the results to be used in Krsna’s service. The *sakama-karma-yogis* lack both faith and knowledge.

Aside from these two types of *bhakti,* there are three categories of devotees: the most intelligent, who learn by hearing; the less intelligent, who cannot learn by hearing but must learn from experience; and the least intelligent who hear but can never act on what they hear. Such persons are punished—that is, they gain experience— but even after punishment they do not learn. We should not be least intelligent. Rather, we should always endeavor to engage in devotional practices in ways that increase our faith in the association of the most intelligent devotees. If we do this, we will eventually come to practice *abhyasa-yoga.*

**Question: What is the proper way to offer activities to the Lord?**

In the *Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu* under *dasya* it is said that there are two types of offerings, *karmarpanam,* or the offering of our activities to the Lord, and *kainkaryam,* the offering of service in the mood of a personal attendant.

The *Skanda Purana* describes *karmarpanam* elaborately and the *Naradiya Purana* alludes briefly to the second, *kainkaryam.* Offering our activities to the Lord *(karmarpanam)* has two subdivisions: offering the fruits of our prescribed duties in *varnasrama,* and offering direct service within the nine main processes of *bhakti.*

Some teachers accept both subdivisions as bona fide forms of servitorship in *sadhana-bhakti.* Our *acaryas,* however, do not. Pure devotional service must be free from work born of fruitive desire. Therefore our acaryas do not accept offering the fruits of our prescribed duties as a bona fide item of *sadhana-bhakti.*

Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura’s commentary to the sixty-first text of *Brahma-samhita* supports this view. He writes that offering our activities in *varnasrama-dharma* is *gauna-bhakti,* subsidiary devotional activity. Such activities are not directly *bhakti,* although they may be conducive to our performance of devotional service. The *Bhagavatam* purport to text 11. 25. 23 states: Ordinary work performed as an offering to God, without desire for the result, is understood to be in the mode of goodness, whereas activities of devotional service—such as chanting and hearing the glories of the Lord—are transcendental forms of work beyond the modes of nature.

And in *The Nectar of Devotion* (p. 95), Srila Prabhupada writes, “In the opinion of the *karmis* (fruitive workers), offering the results of karma is called servitorship. But according to Vaisnava acaryas like Srila Rupa Gosvami, servitorship means constant engagement in some kind of service to the Lord. ”

Srila Prabhupada reconciles these opinions: “Those who are engaged in fruitive activities, or prescribed duties according to the four orders of social and spiritual life, are not actually pure devotees. But still, because they are offering the result to the Lord, they are accepted as devotees. When one has no such desire, but acts spontaneously out of love of God, such a person must be accepted as a pure devotee. ” *(The Nectar of Devotion,* p. 95) (Quoted and paraphrased material from *Waves of Devotion,* by Dhanurdhara Swami (2000))

So there is no benefit if one renounces the activities that are needed to maintain the physical body, thinking them to be the source of misery and lust. The fruits of those activities, which are the source of misery for others, are therefore enjoyed by the devotees, who condemn them as insignificant. They worship Krsna with devotion and firm faith while enjoying the fruits of their work as they fulfill the necessities of life. Those who enjoy the results of material activities with ardent affection are called attached. Those who neglect the results of karma, but accept only what is favorable to devotional service are called unattached. Although they are unattached to material activity, they work enthusiastically in devotional life. The process of advancement for the practitioner of devotional service is stated in the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* (11. 20. 29-30, 35) as follows:

*proktena bhakti-yogena*  
*bhajato masakrn muneh*  
*kama hrdayya nasyanti*  
*sarve mayi hrdi sthite*

*bhidyate hrdaya-granthis*  
*chidyante sarva-samsayah*  
*ksiyante casya karmani*  
*mayi drste ’khilatmani*

*nairapeksyam param prahur*  
*nihsreyasam analpakam*  
*tasman nirasiso bhaktir*  
*nirapeksasya me bhavet*

“When an intelligent person engages constantly in worshiping Me through loving devotional service as described by Me, his heart becomes firmly situated in Me. Thus all material desires within the heart are destroyed. The knot in the heart is pierced, all misgivings are cut to pieces and the chain of fruitive actions is terminated when I am seen as the Supreme Personality of Godhead. It is said that complete detachment is the highest stage of freedom. Therefore, one who has no personal desire and does not pursue personal rewards can achieve loving devotional service unto Me. ”

The purport is this: It is useless trying independently to destroy ignorance and lust in the heart. But ignorance, lust, fruitive activities, all doubts, and material bondage are destroyed by the mercy of the Lord in the course of cultivating devotional service. The endeavor of *karmis* and *jnanis* does not yield such results. Therefore when one becomes neutral by giving up other desires and hopes, he attains pure devotional service to Krsna.

(Sri *Bhaktyaloka,* p. 52)

**Question: How should** *grhasthas* **perform their duties so that they don’t become too entangled, especially if they are dependent on the results of those activities to maintain themselves?**

Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains that householders have to maintain both themselves and their family members. The body should be maintained so that it can be used for hearing and chanting about Krsna. He says that this body is meant for cultivating devotional service. But if we do not work to maintain the body and renounce all these things artificially, then we will untimely expire from our body. This is the consciousness in which we need to work.

The *Bhagavad-gita* (2. 47) states: “You have a right to perform your prescribed duty, but you are not entitled to the fruits of action. Never consider yourself the cause of the results of your activities, and never be attached to not doing your duty. ” Krsna says here that we should come to understand what is our duty and what is not our duty based on the rules and regulations given in *sastra.* Understanding our duty, we should then gradually become spiritually elevated.

Householders have different duties than renunciants. Their duties are to maintain the family and to hear about Krsna every day. Yudhisthira Maharaja told Narada Muni that he was *grha-mudha-dhih,* a householder who has forgotten the point of life. But you, he said, are a *brahmacari.* “Therefore please instruct me in how to live so that I will remember the goal of life. ”

Narada Muni told Yudhisthira that it was the duty of every householder to earn a livelihood and then offer the fruits of his work to Krsna. To remember to do this requires attentive Krsna consciousness. Therefore, every day, the *grhasthas* should hear about the pastimes of the Supreme Personality of Godhead as they have been presented in Vedic literature and in the association of saints. By hearing the Supreme Lord’s pastimes, householders will gradually awaken from conditioned life “exactly like a man awakening from a dream. ” *(Bhag.* 7. 14. 4)

In a dream many things look real, but they have no actual value. If instead of being attentive to our dreams, we give our attention to regularly hearing about the Lord’s pastimes in the association of devotees, then we will awaken to transcendental reality. When we awaken to spiritual truth, we see all duties for what they are—simple necessities to maintain body and soul together. We will also see that the higher goal, to hear and chant constantly about Krsna, is our real duty. Understanding that will encourage us to be detached from the results of our work and instead to offer them to Krsna for His pleasure. We will accept only what is required to facilitate our service. Beyond that, we will use our life to cultivate Krsna consciousness.

Without the association of *sadhus,* it is difficult to remember the goal of life. Therefore work as a matter of duty and take what you need to keep body and soul and family together, but always hanker for nectar. Krsna consciousness is life.

## Parents

*sa esa loke vikhyatah*  
*pariksid iti yat prabhuh*  
*purvam drstam anudhyayan*  
*parikseta naresv iha*

*So* his son would become famous in the world as Pariksit \[examiner\] because he would come to examine all human beings in his search after that personality whom he saw before his birth. Thus he would come to constantly contemplate Him. ” *(Bhag.* 1. 12. 30)

Srila Prabhupada writes in his purport:

Maharaja Pariksit, fortunate as he was, got the impression of the Lord even in the womb of his mother, and thus his contemplation on the Lord was constantly with him. Once the impression of the transcendental form of the Lord is fixed in one’s mind, one can never forget Him in any circumstance. Child Pariksit, after coming out of the womb, was in the habit of examining everyone to see whether he was the same personality whom he first saw in the womb. But no one could be equal to or more attractive than the Lord, and therefore he never accepted anyone. But the Lord was constantly with him by such examination, and thus Maharaja Pariksit was always engaged in the devotional service of the Lord by remembrance.

Srila Jiva Gosvami remarks in this connection that every child, if given an impression of the Lord from his very childhood, certainly becomes a great devotee of the Lord like Maharaja Pariksit. One may not be as fortunate as Maharaja Pariksit to have the opportunity to see the Lord in the womb of his mother, but even if he is not so fortunate, he can be made so if the parents of the child desire him to be so. There is a practical example in my personal life in this connection. My father was a pure devotee of the Lord, and when I was only four or five years old, my father gave me a couple of forms of Radha and Krsna. In a playful manner, I used to worship these deities along with my sister, and I used to imitate the performances of a neighboring temple of Radha-Govinda. By constantly visiting this neighboring temple and copying the ceremonies in connection with my own deities of play, I developed a natural affinity for the Lord. My father used to observe all the ceremonies befitting my position. Later on, these activities were suspended due to my association in the schools and colleges, and I became completely out of practice. But in my youthful days, when I met my spiritual master, Sri Srimad Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Gosvami Maharaja, again I revived my old habit, and the same playful deities became my worshipful deities in proper regulation. This was followed up until I left the family connection, and I am pleased that my generous father gave the first impression which was developed later into regulative devotional service by His Divine Grace. Maharaja Prahlada also advised that such impressions of a godly relation must be impregnated from the beginning of childhood, otherwise one may miss the opportunity of the human form of life, which is very valuable although it is temporary like others.

The scriptures make reference to parental responsibility. Srila Prabhupada gives one such reference here, which can be gathered as evidence to substantiate the importance of the responsibility placed on anyone who desires to be a parent.

Lord Rsabhadeva states that one should not become a king, teacher, parent, or spiritual master unless he or she can deliver the dependent from repeated birth and death. To do so is certainly a tall order. But by delineating the responsibility those who wish to have spiritual dependents must bear, such persons are forced to try to live up to these expectations.

The scriptures list many duties to be performed according to our particular position in life, both social and spiritual. Krsna states in the *Bhagavad-gita* (16. 24), “One should therefore understand what is duty and what is not duty by the regulations of the scriptures. Knowing such rules and regulations, one should act so that he may gradually be elevated. ” Krsna also says that those who act whimsically, neglecting the duties prescribed by scripture, attain “neither perfection, nor happiness, nor the supreme destination. ” (Bg. 16. 23) Therefore we should understand the particular duties we have been assigned according to our status in life and according to the regulations given in scripture.

Srila Prabhupada states that accepting these rules and prohibitions is the actual foundation of human life; it is what distinguishes human beings from animals. Human beings follow certain rules based on higher, sastric authority; those who do not follow these standards are no better than animals. Thus not only do the scriptures teach us who Krsna is and about our relationship with Him, but about the standards of human life. Following these standards elevates our duties from the mundane to the spiritual. Certainly those who become Krsna conscious have no other duties to perform but these. *Srimad-Bhagavatam* (11. 5. 41) states:

*devarsi-bhutapta-nrnam pitrnam*  
*na kinkaro nayam rni ca rajan*  
*sarvatmana yah saranam saranyam*  
*gato mukundam parihrtya kartam*

“O King, one who has given up all material duties and has taken full shelter of the lotus feet of Mukunda, who offers shelter to all, is not indebted to the demigods, great sages, ordinary living beings, relatives, friends, mankind or even one’s forefathers who have passed away. Since all such classes of living entities are part and parcel of the Supreme Lord, one who has surrendered to the Lord’s service has no need to serve such persons separately. ”

And what is the highest duty of every human being? To surrender fully to Krsna. Those who have surrendered to Krsna have fulfilled their highest duty. But such surrender is not cheap, and we should not be quick to assume we have achieved it. Unfortunately, we sometimes use scriptural statements to avoid our responsibility or to justify an irresponsible life. The *Bhagavad-gita* calls such false renunciation renunciation in the mode of passion. Those who in the mode of passion renounce those duties that should actually be done, do so only because they are too difficult. Such renunciation cannot offer a favorable result. We should not therefore reject or avoid duties simply because they are troublesome. And neither should we foolishly equate such renunciation with surrender.

The *acaryas* have clearly delineated the symptoms of surrender. Those who are surrendered are not disturbed in any circumstance; they always accept whatever is favorable for their service to Krsna and reject whatever is unfavorable; they always feel themselves completely dependent on the Lord’s mercy; they have no interest outside the Lord’s interest; and they are always fully confident of the Lord’s protection. Being undisturbed in any situation implies that they are always meek and humble.

We have to examine ourselves to see whether we are situated on this platform. If not, we should be careful when deciding whether or not to renounce our duties, especially the duties prescribed by scripture.

Since we began this discussion speaking about parental responsibility, we should know that parental duties cannot be whimsically rejected, especially in the name of surrender to Krsna. We see in the example that Srila Prabhupada brought to life in his purport that his own father raised him properly in Krsna consciousness. He told us that right from the beginning, his father wanted him to become a servant of Srimati Radharani. When he was young his father gave him deities to worship, and these same deities later became his worshipful Lords. He clearly gives credit to his father for the devotional impressions he received as a young child.

Similarly, Gour Govinda Maharaja was taught to worship the deity Gopala from a young age. His father was also a great devotee of the Lord who always engaged in *kirtana.* Right from his childhood, Gour Govinda Maharaja was surrounded by an atmosphere of constant *kirtana* and deity worship. Therefore, just as with Srila Prabhupada, the deity he worshiped as a child later became his worshipful deity and he established a temple for Him.

Of course, it is not ordinary to take birth in such a devotional family. These parents were devotees themselves and set an example of devotion for their children. At the time of conception they attracted into the mother’s womb highly elevated souls. We may not be able to attract such souls, but we should at least recognize that parental responsibility begins at conception. Parents should want to invite elevated souls into their family so that they can raise such children in Krsna consciousness.

Sometimes we may belittle the duties given in the scriptures. Unfortunately, there is a tendency to think our scriptures are outdated. Devotees think they were written for a different time and that they do not address contemporary life. But if we really look at contemporary life and the people who are living it, we will see that their problems are the same.

Prabhupada often made the point that the *sastra* is relevant to all times because it presents the truth. By definition, truth means it is always true. Prabhupada acknowledged that we may see things in scripture that we cannot accept or that appear contradictory to us, but if we fully and properly analyze it, we will see that the truth is being presented.

There are many statements in scripture that explain what happens if parents do not accept responsibility. Arjuna even mentions the problem in *Bhagavad-gita* where he speaks about the responsibility men have toward their wives and ultimately toward society:

When irreligion is prominent in the family, O Krsna, the women of the family become polluted, and from the degradation of womanhood, O descendant of Vrsni, comes unwanted progeny.

An increase of unwanted population certainly causes hellish life both for the family and for those who destroy the family tradition.

The ancestors of such corrupt families fall down, because the performances for offering them food and water are entirely stopped. (Bg. 1. 40-1)

Human society, Prabhupada explains, is not meant to resemble animal society. Animals procreate without any care or morality and simply produce more animals. Human society should not produce children who engage only in the animal behaviors of eating, sleeping, mating, and defending with no higher purpose or understanding.

To avoid human beings giving in to their lower natures, therefore, and to create peace and religion in society, the scriptures prescribe duties for a parent raising children. The parents have the responsibility of supplying an atmosphere in which moral and spiritual impressions can be created in the child so that the child will become a moral and spiritually advanced adult. Prabhupada mentions some of these impressions in his purport to *Bhagavatam* 1. 12. 30: Deity worship, *kirtana,* and of course, the example of Krsna conscious parents.

Some of our ISKCON youth have been asked why they have maintained their connection with Krsna consciousness despite everything they have been through. Their answer is usually the same: their parents set a good example and encouraged them in spiritual life. Generally, mothers leave strong impressions on boys and fathers on girls. Modern family therapists make the same point— that example is more powerful than precept. They also say that the parents staying together is vital to the health and happiness of the child. Statistics have been compiled on what happens to children whose parents do not stay together, and the results are frightening. Maintaining an intact family is the responsibility of Krsna conscious parents. Sometimes, separation or divorce cannot be avoided, but devotees should avoid such inevitabilities as much as possible. It is so important for children that their parents stay together.

Intact families create stability in a child’s life, but beyond stability, devotee parents want to give their children Krsna consciousness. Krsna is all-attractive. If somehow or other children can form loving impressions of Him from the beginning of their lives, there is hope that even if they leave ISKCON, they may not leave Krsna, and they may someday return to this institution no matter what they have been through. We should be careful not to encourage children to leave Krsna consciousness by our behavior.

Srila Prabhupada writes in his purport to Bhagavatam 1. 12. 30, “Later on, these activities were suspended due to my association in the schools and colleges. ” Prabhupada’s early childhood activities were suspended because of his association, and he therefore “became completely out of practice. ” He tells us that he stopped worshipping his deity, as he did as a child. Still, he met his spiritual master while still a young man, who encouraged him to take his worship up again. Again, the impressions children receive when they are young will help them easily revive their worship, if they leave it aside, when they are older.

From personal observation, I see that children generally remain in Krsna consciousness because of the affection they have received from their parents. Parents should not think, “Let me send my children to *gurukula,* and the *gurukula* will make them devotees, ” and then when the children come home, the parents are engaged in all kinds of nonsense or are distant from their children. This is not the kind of responsibility Lord Rsabhadeva speaks about. No one else will make our children Krsna conscious. Rather, it is our affection for our children that will make them want to stay and practice Krsna consciousness.

Sometimes parents have to discipline their children out of love. Sometimes reprimand is necessary. But the children have to see our love. Otherwise, they will never see our discipline or reprimands for what they are—attempts to guide them for their own good.

Prabhupada himself had children, and when he later had disciples, he showed them affection as if they were his children. He once said, “We left only three or four children; now we have got hundreds of children, without any botheration of wife, (laughter) And they are so obedient and so beautiful, so nice, that I could not expect even the children which I begot at home. ” (Lecture, September 16, 1969)

If you are a parent and have not yet realized the great weight of your responsibility, you must learn for your children’s sake and for the sake of our Krsna consciousness movement. Children too should learn how important their parents’ instruction is, which includes their correction, and should feel their parents’ love. But if we give good love and good Krsna conscious education, as Srila Prabhupada said, we will then see such a joyful outcome in our parenting endeavors.

An intelligent person can learn simply by hearing. Do not be less intelligent. Avoid unnecessary suffering by seeking Krsna conscious solutions. Parents, teachers, the king, and the spiritual master are all meant to help us understand how to apply those solutions. As parents, we are meant to provide such solutions to our children and to apply them ourselves. Make this your last life and then go back to Godhead.

**Question: What should be done when one parent is trying to raise children in Krsna consciousness but the spouse, who is not a devotee, creates opposition?**

That parent should pray very deeply. Obviously, this is a difficult situation—especially for the children, who are pulled in two different directions. There is no particular formula for success; the situation really requires Krsna’s mercy. If we want to invoke Krsna’s mercy, we definitely have to pray, try to set a good example ourselves, and somehow attract the child to see the purity and benefits of living a devotional life as opposed to a demoniac one.

Many years ago I saw an article by Urmila Devi Dasi in *Back to Godhead* about how children who are raised in Krsna consciousness and who are given the choice to see its attractiveness choose Krsna consciousness. But not always. Even Srila Prabhupada’s children did not choose Krsna consciousness. Although Srila Prabhupada was a perfect example throughout his household life, his children did not become devotees. Ultimately, we do not have the power to change our children’s karma. All we can do is show a good example in our own steady practice and to make Krsna consciousness attractive.

In America, because of family law, when parents are divorcing, the child usually becomes a ping-pong ball while the parents fight for custody. One parent may preach the glories of intoxication, illicit sex, gambling, and meat-eating while the other parent preaches the opposite and chants Hare Krsna. Each tries to somehow attract their children. The children go back and forth—one week here, another week there, and in the end, feel like they are in hell.

Somehow or other we have to show our children the attractiveness of Krsna consciousness. And if we really want to help children who are subjected to that kind of exposure, then we ourselves should be attracted to Krsna consciousness. It is only natural that if we are enjoying something, others will want to enjoy it with us. Prabhupada says that young children know only how to imitate. As they grow older, they begin to discriminate. But at the beginning, they imitate. If they see someone loving to chant in the *kirtana,* reading *Krsna* book, and worshipping the deity, they too will want to participate in those activities. But we cannot be putting on a show; children can see through pretense.

## Children

Jiva Gosvami says that the first impressions given to a child last through life. When children are graduating from school, it is time to look back and consider what impressions they have picked up and how to take advantage of them to live out their lives. This is true of any child, but it is especially relevant for those who have grown up in Krsna consciousness.

Srila Prabhupada discusses *sreyas* and *preyas. Sreyas* means ultimate benefit, and *preyas* means immediate benefit. Children tend to be focused on *preyas.* Therefore, they wonder why they should go to school. They would rather go out and play. *Preyas* is considered inauspicious; it leaves one ill-prepared for the future.

Those who think about the ultimate benefit will send their children to school to prepare for the future. Children who come under the care of those who think in terms of *sreyas* forgo some of their play and do not neglect their education.

Of course, children must play some of the time; but Narada Muni set an example of renouncing all frivolous activities for his ultimate good. Narada certainly gathered good impressions from his early childhood because of his association with saintly persons. Those impressions entered deep into his heart, and he was able to devote himself completely to the highest welfare activity. But of course, we do not expect all our children to be as elevated as Narada or Prahlada, so they must play at least some of the time.

Nonetheless, children just entering adulthood should try to understand the principle of *sreyas*—the long-term advantage they will receive if they apply Krsna consciousness into their adult lives. Adulthood is a big responsibility. Children tend not to feel the weight of responsibility, but when they become adults, they understand it more.

Srila Prabhupada often emphasized that human life is meant for responsibility. If a person chooses to live irresponsibly, he or she will be punished by the laws of nature. Therefore we should educate our children so that they know how to discriminate properly and can choose moral and spiritual responsibility.
